## hub\auth.py
- _now (line 126)
- _config_path (line 134)
- config (line 138)
  Doc:   Effective config. The environment wins over the file, deliberately.
  
  If the operator sets forward_auth_mode=strict and locks themselves out,
  the file lives on a VM they may no longer be able to reach *through the
  API*. An env var on the systemd unit is reachable over plain SSH, so it
  overrides the file. See docs/DEVICE_AUTH_MIGRATION.md.
- enrol_code_required (line 193)
  Doc:   Whether POST /api/auth/devices needs a single-use enrolment code right now.
  
  True when the operator switched `require_enrol_code` on, AND whenever
  forward_auth_mode is not `permissive`. Enrolment has to be reachable without
  a token (a device cannot present one before it has one), so it is routed
  around forward_auth; in `permissive` Caddy's basic_auth still stands in
  front of it, but in `basic_or_token` and `strict` that password is gone and
  the code is the only thing between the internet and a new device key. Left
  to the flag alone, `strict` with the flag off meant anyone who could reach
  the URL could enrol a key, sign in with it, and own the relay. An unknown
  mode is treated as `strict` by config(), so it gates too.
- _save_config (line 210)
- _atomic_write_json (line 231)
- _tokens_path (line 243)
- _load_state (line 247)
  Doc:   Rehydrate tokens from disk, once per DATA dir.
  
  Tokens used to live only in a module-level dict, so every hub restart --
  including an unattended `systemctl restart forge-hub` after a deploy --
  logged every device out and demanded a fresh fingerprint prompt on a phone
  that might be nowhere near the operator. Only SHA-256 hashes are stored,
  so reading the file gives you nothing you can present as a bearer token.
- _save_tokens (line 276)
- _client_ip (line 293)
  Doc:   The caller's address as Caddy saw it.
  
  uvicorn binds 127.0.0.1, so request.client.host is always the proxy itself
  and useless as a bucket key. The real address is whatever Caddy put in
  X-Forwarded-For. The LAST entry is the one the proxy itself appended; anything
  before it was supplied by the client, so taking the first (as this used to)
  let a caller dodge every per-IP bucket by prefixing a made-up address. Stock
  Caddy replaces the header outright, so there is only one entry either way.
  
  This still assumes nothing but Caddy can reach the socket: from a directly
  reachable port the whole header is attacker-controlled.
- _too_many (line 316)
- _rate_check (line 324)
  Doc:   Raise 429 if `subject` has spent its allowance in `bucket`.

- _find (line 463)
- _load_public_key (line 470)
  Doc:   Parse a PEM public key and identify its algorithm.
  
  Two are allowed because the platforms differ and neither is negotiable:
    ES256 — Android Keystore/StrongBox issues ECDSA P-256.
    RS256 — Windows Hello (KeyCredentialManager) issues RSA-2048 and signs
            PKCS#1 v1.5 / SHA-256. It cannot be made to produce P-256.
  Anything else is refused rather than guessed at.
  
  Returns (key, alg).
- _basic_credential (line 505)
  Doc:   The hub's own copy of the relay password, as (user, sha256-hex), or None.
  
  Caddy holds the only copy today. Once Caddy stops doing basic auth, *something*
  has to still verify it during migration, or the fallback is not a fallback --
  it is "any Basic header at all". Sourced from the environment first so the
  operator can set it on the systemd unit without writing a secret to disk.
- _basic_file (line 538)
- _plain_bcrypt (line 542)
  Doc:   Older Caddyfiles store the hash base64-wrapped ('JDJh...'). Accept that too,
  so pasting the Caddyfile value unchanged works instead of silently never matching.
- _load_basic (line 554)
  Doc:   `user:bcrypt-hash` lines, reloaded when the file's mtime/size changes.
- _check_basic_file (line 581)
  Doc:   True if the header matches a line in the bcrypt user file.
- _basic_configured (line 620)
  Doc:   Whether ANY basic credential exists — env/JSON single, or the bcrypt file.
- _check_basic (line 625)
  Doc:   True if this Authorization: Basic header matches any configured credential.
- _note_legacy (line 648)
  Doc:   Once a minute per client, record that the shared password was used.
  
  This is the evidence needed before flipping to device-only: when
  `journalctl -u forge-hub | grep 'legacy basic'` goes quiet for every client
  that matters, nothing is still relying on the password. Guessing instead is
  how the phone gets locked out remotely.
- _deny (line 664)
- _decide (line 669)
  Doc:   The single acceptance decision, shared by forward_auth and require_admin.
  
  Three modes, so the switch from "Caddy checks a shared password" to "FastAPI
  checks a device token" is not one all-or-nothing cutover:
  
    permissive    (default) a device token, OR any Basic header. Correct ONLY
                  while Caddy still runs basic_auth in front -- Caddy has
                  already rejected a wrong password, so anything arriving here
                  with a Basic header was vouched for. This is the mode that
                  makes the migration reversible, and the default keeps this
                  whole module additive.
    basic_or_token  a device token, OR a Basic header the hub verifies itself
                  against HUB_BASIC_AUTH_USER/PASSWORD. The mode to run in
                  while Caddy's basic_auth is gone but the desktop app and
                  browser still have no device token. Caddy's bcrypt check no
                  longer sits in front here, so wrong passwords are locked
                  out per client IP (LOCK_BASIC_IP); a valid device token is
                  never affected by that lock.
    strict        a device token, nothing else.
  
  Raises HTTPException on refusal; returns a description of who got in.
- require_admin (line 757)
  Doc:   Guard for the endpoints that can change who gets in.
  
  /api/auth/* has to sit OUTSIDE Caddy's forward_auth -- a device cannot
  present a token before it has one -- so once Caddy stops doing basic auth,
  revoke and config would otherwise be reachable by anyone who found the URL.
  Under `permissive` this is a no-op (Caddy is still checking the password in
  front), which is what keeps the change additive.
- forward_auth (line 772)
  Doc:   Caddy's `forward_auth` target. 2xx lets the request through, 401 stops it.
  
  Success echoes the device back in headers so Caddy can `copy_headers` them
  upstream and the hub can log which device did what.
- _mint_guard (line 786)
  Doc:   Who may mint an enrolment code.
  
  Once ANY device is enrolled, only a device token can. The relay password never
  can: it is exactly the credential a code exists to distinguish from, so a leaked
  password that could also mint its own code would defeat the gate outright
  (probe: with the gate on, a password-holder minted a code and enrolled a device
  in `permissive` and `basic_or_token`).
  
  With zero devices enrolled there is no device credential to ask for, so this
  falls back to `require_admin` (whatever guards the hub). The operator's
  alternative is the CLI on the VM: `python -m auth mint-code`.
- issue_enrol_code (line 814)
  Doc:   Mint a short-lived, single-use enrolment code (see _mint_guard for who may).
- _require_live_code (line 820)
  Doc:   The file of a live enrolment code, or raise. Nothing is spent here.
  
  Wrong codes are counted: LOCK_CODE_IP locks the guesser out, and
  LOCK_CODE_GLOBAL -- guessing spread over many addresses to dodge that -- voids
  every live code and pauses enrolment. A missing code is not a guess and does
  not count.
- enrol (line 857)
  Doc:   Register a device's public key. Guarded by whatever already fronts the hub,
  plus a single-use code once `require_enrol_code` is on.
- get_devices (line 887)
  Doc:   Who is enrolled. Names, ids and last-seen times are nobody's business but the
  operator's, and /api/auth/* can sit outside forward_auth, so this guards itself
  like revoke and config-writes do (a no-op under `permissive`, where Caddy's
  password is still in front).
- revoke (line 896)
  Doc:   Revoke one device without touching the others — the point of the exercise.
- challenge (line 924)
- token (line 950)
  Doc:   Exchange a signature over the outstanding challenge for a short-lived token.
- _hash_token (line 1013)
- resolve_token (line 1019)
  Doc:   Device id for a bearer token, or None. Expired tokens are dropped on read.
  
  A token whose device is no longer enrolled is dead even if nothing purged it (a
  hand-edited devices.json, a tokens.json restored from before a revocation).
  Checked here rather than only in forward auth so that require_device() and
  /whoami -- the things that will guard real routes later -- cannot resurrect a
  revoked device either.
- require_device (line 1045)
  Doc:   FastAPI dependency enforcing a device token. Not yet applied to any route —
  it goes on once both devices are enrolled and Caddy stops doing basic auth.
- whoami (line 1057)
  Doc:   Lets a client check its token without a protected route existing yet.
- get_config (line 1082)
- set_config (line 1101)
- _cli (line 1138)

## hub\test_auth_ports.py
- _keypair (line 27)

  Doc:   A request as Caddy relays it: the real client address in X-Forwarded-For.

- test_a_token_of_a_device_that_is_no_longer_enrolled_is_dead_everywhere (line 116)
  Doc:   Forward auth re-checked enrolment but resolve_token did not, so whoami and the
  require_device() dependency (the thing that will guard real routes) still
  honoured a token whose device had been removed, e.g. by a hand-edited devices.json
  or a tokens.json restored from before a revocation.
- test_concurrent_enrolments_are_all_persisted (line 138)
  Doc:   Read-modify-write without holding the lock across it lost enrolments: every
  caller got a 200 and an id for a device that was not in devices.json.
- test_a_revoke_landing_mid_token_exchange_is_not_undone (line 162)
  Doc:   /token read devices.json, then wrote the whole list back to set last_seen. A
  revoke landing in between was silently reverted, resurrecting the device.
- test_a_revoke_does_not_drop_an_enrolment_that_lands_during_it (line 214)
  Doc:   Same lost-update as the /token case, from the other side: a revoke that read the
  device list, then wrote it back, erased a device enrolled in between.
- test_a_leaked_password_cannot_mint_its_own_enrolment_code (line 256)
  Doc:   The gate was meaningless against the threat it exists for: whoever could reach
  /api/auth (i.e. anyone past Caddy with the relay password) could POST /enrol-code
  and then enrol. Once a device exists, only a device token may mint.
- test_the_first_device_can_still_bootstrap_over_http_when_none_exist (line 284)
  Doc:   Nothing to authenticate against yet, so this falls back to whatever guards the
  hub. The strict path for it is the CLI; this only pins that the door has not been
  welded shut for someone who has no shell.
- test_a_duplicate_key_does_not_burn_the_code (line 294)
- test_an_unparsable_key_does_not_burn_the_code (line 304)
- test_the_code_is_checked_before_the_key_is_parsed (line 313)
  Doc:   The runbook's negative check sends a placeholder key and expects 403. If the key
  were parsed first it would get a 400 and the check could never pass.
- test_racing_enrolments_spend_one_code_exactly_once (line 324)
- test_a_code_minted_by_the_cli_is_spendable_survives_restart_and_is_single_use (line 347)
  Doc:   The CLI is a different process from the running hub, so codes have to live where
  both can see them (they used to be a module-level dict loaded once).
- test_codes_have_no_lookalike_glyphs_and_are_typo_tolerant (line 360)
- test_guessing_codes_locks_the_guesser_but_not_other_addresses (line 368)
- test_distributed_guessing_voids_every_live_code (line 380)
  Doc:   Spreading guesses over addresses dodges any per-IP lock, so wrong codes are also
  counted globally and trip a void of every live code.
- test_a_missing_code_is_not_a_guess (line 393)
  Doc:   An anonymous probe carries no code; counting it would let anyone lock enrolment.
- test_flooding_a_device_id_from_one_address_does_not_starve_the_real_device (line 406)
  Doc:   The per-device buckets were keyed on the device alone, so anyone who knew an id
  could spend its whole allowance from their own address and lock the phone out.
- test_attackers_each_below_their_own_lock_cannot_starve_the_real_device_at_token (line 422)
  Doc:   Three addresses each stop one failure short of the lockout. If the token
  allowance were shared per device, their combined calls would exhaust it.
- test_someone_who_knows_a_device_id_cannot_clobber_its_sign_in_or_get_it_locked_out (line 436)
  Doc:   Challenges were one slot per device, so a caller could replace the phone's nonce
  between its /challenge and /token. Its own signature then failed, and with failures
  locking the (device, IP) pair, five such clobbers locked the phone out.
- test_a_challenge_is_only_good_for_the_address_that_asked_for_it (line 450)
  Doc:   The other side of the same binding: a nonce issued to one address cannot be
  redeemed from another, so a captured nonce plus a stolen signature is not enough.
- test_expired_challenges_do_not_pile_up_when_addresses_are_rotated (line 462)
- test_bad_signatures_lock_the_pair_and_the_lock_holds_against_a_good_one (line 472)
  Doc:   Rate limits only slow a guesser; failures have to lock them out. Locked means
  locked, even for the correct key from that address, until the lock expires.
- test_a_good_login_clears_the_failure_count (line 490)
- test_a_client_prefixed_forwarded_for_entry_does_not_dodge_the_per_ip_limit (line 505)
  Doc:   Only the entry the proxy appended (the last) is trustworthy; earlier ones are
  client-supplied. Keying on the first let a caller mint a new bucket per request.
- test_limiter_memory_is_bounded_when_keys_are_rotated (line 517)
- test_password_guessing_in_basic_or_token_locks_that_address_only_for_basic (line 525)
  Doc:   Moving the password check from Caddy's bcrypt to the hub must not make it an
  unthrottled online guess. The lock is on wrong PASSWORDS, so a device token from
  the same address is never caught in it.
- test_state_files_are_owner_only (line 548)
- test_require_device_is_applied_to_no_existing_route (line 558)
  Doc:   Additive contract: the operator, not this change, decides when routes enforce
  tokens. Walks nested dependencies too, so a wrapper around it is caught.
- test_cli_lists_enrolled_devices (line 569)
- test_cli_revoke_needs_no_credentials_and_kills_the_devices_tokens (line 576)
  Doc:   DELETE /devices/<id> needs credentials in every mode but `permissive`, so the
  runbook's `curl localhost:9000 -X DELETE` 401s in exactly the modes where you would
  reach for it. The CLI needs none, and the running hub must honour it at once.
